========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I know what you're asking, like having a firewall constantly pinging for intrusions or running a broad-spectrum threat scan in the background all day, but that's not really how my brain is operating right now. 

When I'm in what I consider my safe zones—like my living room at home, or even just sitting quietly at my specific workstation, assuming I took my usual safe route to get there and completely avoided the west wing—I actually feel fine. Secure, even. I'm not sitting there looking over my shoulder every five seconds, and I don't check the locks on my front door three or four times at night to make sure nobody is coming in. 

It's really more about avoiding the known bad sectors, like I was explaining before with the hallways and the conversations. If I'm in a controlled environment, I just want to put my head down and focus on my tasks. I don't have this generalized, spinning radar looking for new, random dangers out in the wild. Like, if I'm at the grocery store on a Sunday afternoon, I'm just comparing prices on coffee beans or looking at produce. I'm not calculating the fastest escape routes out of the dairy aisle, and I'm not eyeing every single person who walks past my cart thinking they're going to rush at me. I just want to buy my groceries and go home in peace. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout everywhere I go. My... my threat detection, I guess you could call it, is just highly, highly specific to that one night and the things directly associated with it. As long as I'm nowhere near that parking lot, and nobody is getting animated on a conference call or talking about the overnight contractor crew, my system isn't actively searching for danger. I just try to stay in my lane and keep things as predictable as humanly possible.

I just, I don't know what's getting into me. \n\nLike, at work, we'll be in the middle of a major system deployment—which, if you know anything about IT, is the absolute most critical time, everybody is stressed, the servers are down, and you have to be at your desk—and I'll just walk out. And that is just... that is not how I’ve ever handled stress before. Ever. I usually just put my head down and work through things, so the fact that I'm acting so reckless and self-destructive is really freaking me out. I just felt like I had to come in and talk to someone before I completely ruin my life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually anxious or on edge lately?\n\nPatient: Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It’s like... it's like my nerves are completely frayed, just stripped bare. And I don't mean just a little flinch. I mean I jerk so hard my knee actually smashed into the underside of my desk the other day. And it's not just loud noises, either. If someone just walks up behind my chair—which happens all the time, you know, people just pop over to ask about a support ticket or a server issue—or if my desk phone suddenly rings... I mean, I even turned the ringer volume down as low as it goes, but when it goes off, I practically leap out of my skin. \n\nAnd then my hands start shaking. Like, literally trembling over the keyboard, to the point where I have to pull them down into my lap and just grip my fingers together tightly so my coworkers don't notice what's happening to me. It can take me several minutes, just sitting there taking these deep breaths, to finally look normal again and be able to type. I'm sitting there thinking, 'It's just a phone, why are you reacting like this?' but my body just completely overreacts before my logical brain can even process what the sound was. It's just exhausting being this jumpy all the time, always waiting for the next thing to make me jump.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, intrusive thoughts lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. And it's... it's incredibly disruptive. I'll just be sitting there, right? Just normal office stuff. But out of nowhere, completely unprovoked, I'll get hit with these images. Just these flashes from that night. \n\nAnd it's not just pictures in my head, it's the sounds, too. The specific sounds of it. They come flooding back so vividly and so strongly that my mind just completely derails. I'll be mid-sentence, literally explaining a data migration protocol, and then bam, I'm just... gone. I completely lose track of what I was saying. I'll just trail off and stare blankly at my team, and they're all looking at me like I've lost my mind, waiting for me to finish my thought. And I can't even remember what the thought was because my brain is just entirely hijacked by what happened. It's like a movie playing in my head that I can't pause or turn off, and I just have to stand there and wait for it to pass while everyone is staring at me. It's humiliating, really, because I pride myself on being articulate and sharp, and now I'm just... glitching out in the middle of meetings.\n\nDoctor: Is the night you're referring to, with the images and sounds that come flooding back, related to a specific traumatic event that happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, it is. It's... well, it's from this one specific night. A late-night cutover incident. We call them cutovers in IT, it's when you transition from an old system to a new one, and you always do it in the middle of the night on a weekend when user traffic is at its lowest, to minimize impact if things go wrong. Which they always do, but... anyway. \n\nIt was a little over a year ago. About 13 months now, to be exact. I can tell you exactly how long it's been because it feels like my life is divided into two parts now—before that night, and after. I had been working at the office until maybe 2:00 AM. We had hit a major snag with the database migration, and I was the lead analyst, so I couldn't leave until the servers were stable. I finally got things running, packed up my laptop bag, and headed out to my car. Our office park is in this slightly industrial area, so it's completely deserted at that hour. Just empty parking lots and those awful, flickering orange streetlights. \n\nI was just walking to my car, exhausted, just thinking about getting home and finally sleeping, and... well, that's when it happened. That's the night I keep flashing back to. The sounds, the images, everything. It's been 13 months of this, and it honestly feels like it happened yesterday. The way my brain just keeps looping it, over and over, every single day since that cutover. It's just... it's been a year of this, and it's not getting any better.\n\nDoctor: What happened to you that night as you were walking to your car?\n\nPatient: Well, I was parked out in the visitor lot, which is a bit further away, because they were repaving the main employee section that week. So I was walking, just carrying my heavy laptop bag, and I heard this... this noise. And I turned around, and... \n\nIt's so frustrating to explain, because I can remember some parts of what happened next so incredibly clearly. Vividly. Like it’s burned into my retinas. It was one of the overnight contractors—we had a crew doing some extensive HVAC work in the adjacent building at the time. I recognized his face immediately. I can still see his face perfectly, the exact expression he had, the stubble on his jaw, even the way the orange streetlight hit his jacket. And the noise—God, the noise of him rushing at me, the heavy crunch of his boots on the loose gravel, the sound of my own bag hitting the pavement. It's all so loud in my head.\n\nBut then... it's like a corrupted hard drive. After that initial moment, there’s just this massive chunk in the middle of the attack where my mind just goes completely black. I know I was there, obviously. I know things were happening to me, I know there was a struggle, but I just... I can’t pull it back. I try. Believe me, I sit awake at night and I try to force my brain to retrieve the missing data, to connect point A to point C, but point B is just gone. Completely wiped. \n\nI remember the start, his face, that terrifying noise of him coming at me, and then the very next thing I actually remember is just... being on the ground next to my front tire, freezing cold, bleeding, with him running off. But that middle part? The actual worst of it? It’s just a void. And it drives me absolutely insane because my entire career, my whole life, is based on putting pieces of information together and finding the missing data, but I can't even do it with my own memory. I just can't access it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities that remind you of that night?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. Constantly, actually. It's... it's become this whole exhausting routine that I have to map out every single day before I even leave my house. Like, I used to just walk into work, you know? Just park, walk in, grab coffee, go to my desk. But now? Now it's this whole logistical nightmare. \n\nFor one thing, I absolutely refuse to go anywhere near that side of the building. I mean, I will literally take the long way around the entire complex. If it's raining, if it's snowing, I don't care. I will park on the complete opposite side of the campus, in the overflow lot, and walk an extra fifteen minutes just so I don't have to look at the visitor lot or that patch of gravel where it happened. And the building itself—that adjacent building where the HVAC crew was working? I won't even look at it. I avoid that entire floor of our main building, too, the one with the windows facing it, whenever I possibly can. If someone schedules a meeting in one of the conference rooms on that side, I'll make up an excuse to dial in remotely from my desk, or I'll say I have a conflict. I just... I can't be over there. It makes my skin crawl. \n\nAnd it's not just the physical places, either. It's the people and the... the topics of conversation. In IT, people talk about past projects all the time, right? \"Oh, remember the 2022 cutover? Remember how bad that migration was?\" And the moment—the very second—someone brings up that specific cutover, or mentions overnight contractors, or HVAC work, I just shut it down. I will literally interrupt them, change the subject completely, or just get up and walk away. I'll say I need to check on a server status or grab some water. I can't listen to it. Even if they're just complaining about the air conditioning being too cold and mentioning the contractors who fixed it, my heart starts pounding, my hands get sweaty, and I just have to escape the conversation. It's like I have to build this massive firewall around my entire life just to keep anything related to that night out, and it takes so much energy. It's just so draining to constantly be on guard like that, mapping out my routes, policing conversations... but if I don't do it, I just feel like I'm going to fall apart right there in the middle of the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, especially when you're reminded of that night?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like an out-of-body experience, right? Where you're floating above yourself looking down, almost like a security camera? I've read about that, how the brain sometimes does that to protect itself, like a fail-safe mechanism, disconnecting the user from the hardware, so to speak. But for me... no. I don't get that at all. \n\nIf anything, I feel entirely too trapped *inside* my body. Like, when someone mentions the cutover, or I accidentally hear gravel crunching, I don't float away or detach. I am painfully aware of every single physical sensation. My heart starts hammering so hard I can feel it in my teeth, and my palms get incredibly sweaty—like I was saying before, when I have to go get water or pretend to check a server just to hide how much I'm shaking. So, no, I'm definitely not watching myself from the outside. I'm very much stuck on the inside, feeling every awful bit of it. \n\nIt's like I'm hyper-connected to my body, not disconnected. I almost wish I could just unplug and float away sometimes, honestly, because feeling all that adrenaline and terror rushing through my veins every time I get reminded of that night is just exhausting. It just drains my battery down to zero, having to physically feel all of that over and over again. But no, I've never had that watching-myself feeling. I'm always just right here.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of that night?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes I do. It's... it's honestly one of the most disruptive parts of my day. You know, I was talking earlier about how I try to avoid that side of the building, the conference rooms and all that? Well, sometimes I just can't avoid it, or sometimes it doesn't even matter where I am, it just happens completely out of nowhere. Like, absolutely unprompted. No warning, no error message, just—boom. \n\nI'll be sitting there, maybe in that specific conference area if I absolutely had to go in for a mandatory all-hands, or even just sitting safely at my own desk on a routine conference call. And if someone... if someone just raises their voice on the call, you know? Not even yelling at me, just getting a little too animated about a project deadline or a server patch or whatever, it's like a switch flips in my head. \n\nSuddenly, I get hit with these images and sounds from that night, and it's not just like casually remembering something, it's so incredibly strong. It's like I'm right back in the lot. I hear the gravel crunching, I see the orange streetlight on his jacket, I hear that awful noise of him rushing at me—all those things I told you about earlier, they just flood my system all at once. It's so intense, so loud and vivid, that it completely overrides whatever is actually happening in reality. \n\nAnd I'll be right in the middle of a sentence, explaining a system architecture or giving a status update to my team, and I just... I completely lose track of what I was saying. My mind just drops the connection to the present because it's so overwhelmed by the images from the parking lot. I'll just stop talking, right mid-sentence. And then there's this awful, awkward silence on the call, and people are saying, \"Hello? Are you still there? Did we lose you?\" And I have to scramble to pretend it was a network lag or my headset disconnected, because how do you explain to your project managers that you just spent the last ten seconds terrified out of your mind because of a sound only you can hear? It's just so frustrating, this constant intrusion into my work. I just want to do my job, but my brain keeps forcing these pop-ups of that night that I can't close, and it takes me so long to reboot and figure out where I even was in the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid expressing or feeling certain emotions because they remind you of that night?\n\nPatient: Emotions? Well... I guess, yes, but it's... it's all tied together with what I was telling you earlier. I don't know if I consciously say to myself, \"I'm going to avoid feeling scared today,\" but I do absolutely everything in my power to prevent those specific feelings from bubbling up. Because when they do, it's just too much. It's a complete system overload. \n\nSo, to keep those emotions offline, to stop myself from feeling that absolute, paralyzing terror and vulnerability again, I have to rely on all those workarounds I mentioned. That's exactly why I take the long way around the building, you know? And why I avoid that floor whenever I possibly can. It's not just about the physical space itself, it's about avoiding the intense dread that completely washes over me if I get even a few feet too close to that side of the campus. If I don't see the gravel, if I don't look out those windows, I don't have to feel the panic. I can just keep it suppressed. \n\nAnd it's the exact same principle with the people at work and the things they say. That's the whole reason I shut down conversations the moment people start talking about the contractor or the cutover. I mean, the absolute second the words leave their mouths, I have to stop it. Because if I let them keep talking, if I just sit there politely and listen to them complain about the overnight shift or the HVAC repairs, I'm going to start feeling all of it again. My chest will tighten, and all that fear just comes rushing back in. \n\nI just can't afford to feel those things while I'm trying to do my job, while I'm trying to run diagnostics or lead a project update. It's just not sustainable to be emotionally compromised like that in a professional environment. So, I guess my way of avoiding the emotions is by rigorously avoiding the triggers. It's like I have to perfectly control my environment, down to the exact hallway I walk down and the exact topics my coworkers are allowed to discuss around me, just to keep those feelings locked away in a box. Because if the box opens... well, I just can't let it open. So yes, I avoid the feelings by strictly policing where I go and what I listen to, even though it takes up so much of my mental bandwidth every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering important parts of what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, and it is incredibly frustrating for someone in my line of work. Because, you know, my entire job is about tracking logs, finding the root cause, mapping out processes from start to finish without any dropped packets. But with that night... it's like there's a corrupted sector in my memory. \n\nI can remember some parts of what happened so, so clearly. I mean, painfully clearly. Like I said before, the noise of that gravel crunching, the exact look on the contractor’s face, the way the orange light hit him when he rushed at me... those details are burned into my retinas like a monitor that’s been left on too long. I can see and hear all of that in absolute high definition. It's perfectly preserved. But then... then there’s just this gap. \n\nThere is a whole chunk in the middle of the event where I know I was there, obviously, because I'm sitting here talking to you now, but I just... I can’t pull it back. It’s like the system went offline and just stopped writing data for a few minutes. I try, I really do. I'll be lying in bed, or sitting at my desk when I'm supposed to be running reports, just trying to force the timeline to make sense, trying to connect point A to point C. But point B is just a black hole. \n\nI know time passed, I know things happened in that middle chunk, but no matter how hard I try to query my own brain, I get nothing. A complete retrieval error. It drives me absolutely crazy because I hate not knowing, I hate losing control of the data. I hate that my own mind just seemingly deleted or encrypted a portion of my life, even if it was a terrible portion. I know I was there, but I just can't access it no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I know what you're asking, like having a firewall constantly pinging for intrusions or running a broad-spectrum threat scan in the background all day, but that's not really how my brain is operating right now. \n\nWhen I'm in what I consider my safe zones—like my living room at home, or even just sitting quietly at my specific workstation, assuming I took my usual safe route to get there and completely avoided the west wing—I actually feel fine. Secure, even. I'm not sitting there looking over my shoulder every five seconds, and I don't check the locks on my front door three or four times at night to make sure nobody is coming in. \n\nIt's really more about avoiding the known bad sectors, like I was explaining before with the hallways and the conversations. If I'm in a controlled environment, I just want to put my head down and focus on my tasks. I don't have this generalized, spinning radar looking for new, random dangers out in the wild. Like, if I'm at the grocery store on a Sunday afternoon, I'm just comparing prices on coffee beans or looking at produce. I'm not calculating the fastest escape routes out of the dairy aisle, and I'm not eyeing every single person who walks past my cart thinking they're going to rush at me. I just want to buy my groceries and go home in peace. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout everywhere I go. My... my threat detection, I guess you could call it, is just highly, highly specific to that one night and the things directly associated with it. As long as I'm nowhere near that parking lot, and nobody is getting animated on a conference call or talking about the overnight contractor crew, my system isn't actively searching for danger. I just try to stay in my lane and keep things as predictable as humanly possible.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can just keep it suppressed. \n\nAnd it's the exact same principle with the people at work and the things they say. That's the whole reason I shut down conversations the moment people start talking about the contractor or the cutover. I mean, the absolute second the words leave their mouths, I have to stop it. Because if I let them keep talking, if I just sit there politely and listen to them complain about the overnight shift or the HVAC repairs, I'm going to start feeling all of it again. My chest will tighten, and all that fear just comes rushing back in. \n\nI just can't afford to feel those things while I'm trying to do my job, while I'm trying to run diagnostics or lead a project update. It's just not sustainable to be emotionally compromised like that in a professional environment. So, I guess my way of avoiding the emotions is by rigorously avoiding the triggers. It's like I have to perfectly control my environment, down to the exact hallway I walk down and the exact topics my coworkers are allowed to discuss around me, just to keep those feelings locked away in a box. Because if the box opens... well, I just can't let it open. So yes, I avoid the feelings by strictly policing where I go and what I listen to, even though it takes up so much of my mental bandwidth every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering important parts of what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, and it is incredibly frustrating for someone in my line of work. Because, you know, my entire job is about tracking logs, finding the root cause, mapping out processes from start to finish without any dropped packets. But with that night... it's like there's a corrupted sector in my memory. \n\nI can remember some parts of what happened so, so clearly. I mean, painfully clearly. Like I said before, the noise of that gravel crunching, the exact look on the contractor’s face, the way the orange light hit him when he rushed at me... those details are burned into my retinas like a monitor that’s been left on too long. I can see and hear all of that in absolute high definition. It's perfectly preserved. But then... then there’s just this gap. \n\nThere is a whole chunk in the middle of the event where I know I was there, obviously, because I'm sitting here talking to you now, but I just... I can’t pull it back. It’s like the system went offline and just stopped writing data for a few minutes. I try, I really do. I'll be lying in bed, or sitting at my desk when I'm supposed to be running reports, just trying to force the timeline to make sense, trying to connect point A to point C. But point B is just a black hole. \n\nI know time passed, I know things happened in that middle chunk, but no matter how hard I try to query my own brain, I get nothing. A complete retrieval error. It drives me absolutely crazy because I hate not knowing, I hate losing control of the data. I hate that my own mind just seemingly deleted or encrypted a portion of my life, even if it was a terrible portion. I know I was there, but I just can't access it no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I know what you're asking, like having a firewall constantly pinging for intrusions or running a broad-spectrum threat scan in the background all day, but that's not really how my brain is operating right now. \n\nWhen I'm in what I consider my safe zones—like my living room at home, or even just sitting quietly at my specific workstation, assuming I took my usual safe route to get there and completely avoided the west wing—I actually feel fine. Secure, even. I'm not sitting there looking over my shoulder every five seconds, and I don't check the locks on my front door three or four times at night to make sure nobody is coming in. \n\nIt's really more about avoiding the known bad sectors, like I was explaining before with the hallways and the conversations. If I'm in a controlled environment, I just want to put my head down and focus on my tasks. I don't have this generalized, spinning radar looking for new, random dangers out in the wild. Like, if I'm at the grocery store on a Sunday afternoon, I'm just comparing prices on coffee beans or looking at produce. I'm not calculating the fastest escape routes out of the dairy aisle, and I'm not eyeing every single person who walks past my cart thinking they're going to rush at me. I just want to buy my groceries and go home in peace. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout everywhere I go. My... my threat detection, I guess you could call it, is just highly, highly specific to that one night and the things directly associated with it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep? Oh, absolutely. It's... it's a complete mess right now. You know how you're supposed to just power down at the end of the day? Close out all the background applications and just rest? My brain just refuses to execute that command. I'll get into bed, usually around 11:00, which used to be my standard, reliable time, but instead of drifting off, I just lie there. And I mean I lie there for hours. Staring at the ceiling, trying to focus on the ceiling fan or the dark room, but my mind just starts running these unwanted processes, constantly replaying pieces of that office incident over and over again. It's like a log file stuck in an infinite loop, just cycling through the details of what happened, turning it over and over. 

And then, when my system finally does crash—when I actually manage to fall asleep just out of sheer physical exhaustion—it's not even a restful state. It's totally fragmented. I'll jolt awake, completely tense, my shoulders up by my ears, my muscles just locked up. Sometimes it's because of a dream, though I don't always remember the exact narrative, just that awful, lingering feeling of panic. Other times, it's just from some tiny, insignificant noise in the house. The ice maker in the fridge dropping ice, the HVAC kicking on, or just the floorboards settling. Just a tiny little ping in the environment, and I'm instantly awake again, completely wired and staring at the clock. 

Because of all that, by the time my actual alarm goes off in the morning, I am just utterly depleted. I'm exhausted before work even starts. I have to drag myself out of bed and try to force down coffee just to get enough baseline energy to look at my monitors and do my job. But it's like starting the workday with my battery already at five percent. It's incredibly frustrating because I know I need the downtime to function properly, to process data and solve problems, but my sleep cycle is just completely broken since that night.

I just, I don't know what's getting into me. \n\nLike, at work, we'll be in the middle of a major system deployment—which, if you know anything about IT, is the absolute most critical time, everybody is stressed, the servers are down, and you have to be at your desk—and I'll just walk out. And that is just... that is not how I’ve ever handled stress before. Ever. Constantly. It’s like... it's like my nerves are completely frayed, just stripped bare. And I don't mean just a little flinch. I mean I jerk so hard my knee actually smashed into the underside of my desk the other day. And it's not just loud noises, either. If someone just walks up behind my chair—which happens all the time, you know, people just pop over to ask about a support ticket or a server issue—or if my desk phone suddenly rings... I mean, I even turned the ringer volume down as low as it goes, but when it goes off, I practically leap out of my skin. \n\nAnd then my hands start shaking. Like, literally trembling over the keyboard, to the point where I have to pull them down into my lap and just grip my fingers together tightly so my coworkers don't notice what's happening to me. It can take me several minutes, just sitting there taking these deep breaths, to finally look normal again and be able to type. I'm sitting there thinking, 'It's just a phone, why are you reacting like this?' but my body just completely overreacts before my logical brain can even process what the sound was. It's just exhausting being this jumpy all the time, always waiting for the next thing to make me jump.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any recurring, intrusive thoughts lately?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. And it's... it's incredibly disruptive. I'll just be sitting there, right? Maybe I'm in the middle of a meeting in the main conference area, trying to explain a new patch deployment, or I'm on a conference call and someone just... someone just raises their voice a little bit in frustration about a bug. Just normal office stuff. But out of nowhere, completely unprovoked, I'll get hit with these images. Just these flashes from that night. \n\nAnd it's not just pictures in my head, it's the sounds, too. The specific sounds of it. They come flooding back so vividly and so strongly that my mind just completely derails. I'll be mid-sentence, literally explaining a data migration protocol, and then bam, I'm just... gone. I completely lose track of what I was saying. I'll just trail off and stare blankly at my team, and they're all looking at me like I've lost my mind, waiting for me to finish my thought. And I can't even remember what the thought was because my brain is just entirely hijacked by what happened. It's like a movie playing in my head that I can't pause or turn off, and I just have to stand there and wait for it to pass while everyone is staring at me. It's humiliating, really, because I pride myself on being articulate and sharp, and now I'm just... glitching out in the middle of meetings.\n\nDoctor: Is the night you're referring to, with the images and sounds that come flooding back, related to a specific traumatic event that happened to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, it is. It's... well, it's from this one specific night. A late-night cutover incident. We call them cutovers in IT, it's when you transition from an old system to a new one, and you always do it in the middle of the night on a weekend when user traffic is at its lowest, to minimize impact if things go wrong. Which they always do, but... anyway. \n\nIt was a little over a year ago. About 13 months now, to be exact. I can tell you exactly how long it's been because it feels like my life is divided into two parts now—before that night, and after. I had been working at the office until maybe 2:00 AM. We had hit a major snag with the database migration, and I was the lead analyst, so I couldn't leave until the servers were stable. I finally got things running, packed up my laptop bag, and headed out to my car. Our office park is in this slightly industrial area, so it's completely deserted at that hour. Just empty parking lots and those awful, flickering orange streetlights. \n\nI was just walking to my car, exhausted, just thinking about getting home and finally sleeping, and... well, that's when it happened. That's the night I keep flashing back to. The sounds, the images, everything. It's been 13 months of this, and it honestly feels like it happened yesterday. The way my brain just keeps looping it, over and over, every single day since that cutover. It's just... it's been a year of this, and it's not getting any better.\n\nDoctor: What happened to you that night as you were walking to your car?\n\nPatient: Well, I was parked out in the visitor lot, which is a bit further away, because they were repaving the main employee section that week. So I was walking, just carrying my heavy laptop bag, and I heard this... this noise. And I turned around, and... \n\nIt's so frustrating to explain, because I can remember some parts of what happened next so incredibly clearly. Vividly. Like it’s burned into my retinas. It was one of the overnight contractors—we had a crew doing some extensive HVAC work in the adjacent building at the time. I recognized his face immediately. I can still see his face perfectly, the exact expression he had, the stubble on his jaw, even the way the orange streetlight hit his jacket. And the noise—God, the noise of him rushing at me, the heavy crunch of his boots on the loose gravel, the sound of my own bag hitting the pavement. It's all so loud in my head.\n\nBut then... it's like a corrupted hard drive. After that initial moment, there’s just this massive chunk in the middle of the attack where my mind just goes completely black. I know I was there, obviously. I know things were happening to me, I know there was a struggle, but I just... I can’t pull it back. I try. Believe me, I sit awake at night and I try to force my brain to retrieve the missing data, to connect point A to point C, but point B is just gone. Completely wiped. \n\nI remember the start, his face, that terrifying noise of him coming at me, and then the very next thing I actually remember is just... being on the ground next to my front tire, freezing cold, bleeding, with him running off. But that middle part? The actual worst of it? It’s just a void. And it drives me absolutely insane because my entire career, my whole life, is based on putting pieces of information together and finding the missing data, but I can't even do it with my own memory. I just can't access it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, people, or activities that remind you of that night?\n\nPatient: Well, yes. Yes, I do. Constantly, actually. It's... it's become this whole exhausting routine that I have to map out every single day before I even leave my house. Like, I used to just walk into work, you know? Just park, walk in, grab coffee, go to my desk. But now? Now it's this whole logistical nightmare. \n\nFor one thing, I absolutely refuse to go anywhere near that side of the building. I mean, I will literally take the long way around the entire complex. If it's raining, if it's snowing, I don't care. I will park on the complete opposite side of the campus, in the overflow lot, and walk an extra fifteen minutes just so I don't have to look at the visitor lot or that patch of gravel where it happened. And the building itself—that adjacent building where the HVAC crew was working? I won't even look at it. I avoid that entire floor of our main building, too, the one with the windows facing it, whenever I possibly can. If someone schedules a meeting in one of the conference rooms on that side, I'll make up an excuse to dial in remotely from my desk, or I'll say I have a conflict. I just... I can't be over there. It makes my skin crawl. \n\nAnd it's not just the physical places, either. It's the people and the... the topics of conversation. In IT, people talk about past projects all the time, right? \"Oh, remember the 2022 cutover? Remember how bad that migration was?\" And the moment—the very second—someone brings up that specific cutover, or mentions overnight contractors, or HVAC work, I just shut it down. I will literally interrupt them, change the subject completely, or just get up and walk away. I'll say I need to check on a server status or grab some water. I can't listen to it. Even if they're just complaining about the air conditioning being too cold and mentioning the contractors who fixed it, my heart starts pounding, my hands get sweaty, and I just have to escape the conversation. It's like I have to build this massive firewall around my entire life just to keep anything related to that night out, and it takes so much energy. It's just so draining to constantly be on guard like that, mapping out my routes, policing conversations... but if I don't do it, I just feel like I'm going to fall apart right there in the middle of the office.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, especially when you're reminded of that night?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't think so. I mean, I know what you're talking about, like an out-of-body experience, right? Where you're floating above yourself looking down, almost like a security camera? I've read about that, how the brain sometimes does that to protect itself, like a fail-safe mechanism, disconnecting the user from the hardware, so to speak. But for me... no. I don't get that at all. \n\nIf anything, I feel entirely too trapped *inside* my body. Like, when someone mentions the cutover, or I accidentally hear gravel crunching, I don't float away or detach. I am painfully aware of every single physical sensation. My heart starts hammering so hard I can feel it in my teeth, and my palms get incredibly sweaty—like I was saying before, when I have to go get water or pretend to check a server just to hide how much I'm shaking. So, no, I'm definitely not watching myself from the outside. I'm very much stuck on the inside, feeling every awful bit of it. \n\nIt's like I'm hyper-connected to my body, not disconnected. I almost wish I could just unplug and float away sometimes, honestly, because feeling all that adrenaline and terror rushing through my veins every time I get reminded of that night is just exhausting. It just drains my battery down to zero, having to physically feel all of that over and over again. But no, I've never had that watching-myself feeling. I'm always just right here.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of that night?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes I do. It's... it's honestly one of the most disruptive parts of my day. You know, I was talking earlier about how I try to avoid that side of the building, the conference rooms and all that? Well, sometimes I just can't avoid it, or sometimes it doesn't even matter where I am, it just happens completely out of nowhere. Like, absolutely unprompted. No warning, no error message, just—boom. \n\nI'll be sitting there, maybe in that specific conference area if I absolutely had to go in for a mandatory all-hands, or even just sitting safely at my own desk on a routine conference call. And if someone... if someone just raises their voice on the call, you know? Not even yelling at me, just getting a little too animated about a project deadline or a server patch or whatever, it's like a switch flips in my head. \n\nSuddenly, I get hit with these images and sounds from that night, and it's not just like casually remembering something, it's so incredibly strong. It's like I'm right back in the lot. I hear the gravel crunching, I see the orange streetlight on his jacket, I hear that awful noise of him rushing at me—all those things I told you about earlier, they just flood my system all at once. It's so intense, so loud and vivid, that it completely overrides whatever is actually happening in reality. \n\nAnd I'll be right in the middle of a sentence, explaining a system architecture or giving a status update to my team, and I just... I completely lose track of what I was saying. My mind just drops the connection to the present because it's so overwhelmed by the images from the parking lot. I'll just stop talking, right mid-sentence. And then there's this awful, awkward silence on the call, and people are saying, \"Hello? Are you still there? Did we lose you?\" And I have to scramble to pretend it was a network lag or my headset disconnected, because how do you explain to your project managers that you just spent the last ten seconds terrified out of your mind because of a sound only you can hear? It's just so frustrating, this constant intrusion into my work. I just want to do my job, but my brain keeps forcing these pop-ups of that night that I can't close, and it takes me so long to reboot and figure out where I even was in the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid expressing or feeling certain emotions because they remind you of that night?\n\nPatient: Emotions? Well... I guess, yes, but it's... it's all tied together with what I was telling you earlier. I don't know if I consciously say to myself, \"I'm going to avoid feeling scared today,\" but I do absolutely everything in my power to prevent those specific feelings from bubbling up. Because when they do, it's just too much. It's a complete system overload. \n\nSo, to keep those emotions offline, to stop myself from feeling that absolute, paralyzing terror and vulnerability again, I have to rely on all those workarounds I mentioned. That's exactly why I take the long way around the building, you know? And why I avoid that floor whenever I possibly can. It's not just about the physical space itself, it's about avoiding the intense dread that completely washes over me if I get even a few feet too close to that side of the campus. If I don't see the gravel, if I don't look out those windows, I don't have to feel the panic. I can just keep it suppressed. \n\nAnd it's the exact same principle with the people at work and the things they say. That's the whole reason I shut down conversations the moment people start talking about the contractor or the cutover. I mean, the absolute second the words leave their mouths, I have to stop it. Because if I let them keep talking, if I just sit there politely and listen to them complain about the overnight shift or the HVAC repairs, I'm going to start feeling all of it again. My chest will tighten, and all that fear just comes rushing back in. \n\nI just can't afford to feel those things while I'm trying to do my job, while I'm trying to run diagnostics or lead a project update. It's just not sustainable to be emotionally compromised like that in a professional environment. So, I guess my way of avoiding the emotions is by rigorously avoiding the triggers. It's like I have to perfectly control my environment, down to the exact hallway I walk down and the exact topics my coworkers are allowed to discuss around me, just to keep those feelings locked away in a box. Because if the box opens... well, I just can't let it open. So yes, I avoid the feelings by strictly policing where I go and what I listen to, even though it takes up so much of my mental bandwidth every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering important parts of what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, and it is incredibly frustrating for someone in my line of work. Because, you know, my entire job is about tracking logs, finding the root cause, mapping out processes from start to finish without any dropped packets. But with that night... it's like there's a corrupted sector in my memory. \n\nI can remember some parts of what happened so, so clearly. I mean, painfully clearly. Like I said before, the noise of that gravel crunching, the exact look on the contractor’s face, the way the orange light hit him when he rushed at me... those details are burned into my retinas like a monitor that’s been left on too long. I can see and hear all of that in absolute high definition. It's perfectly preserved. But then... then there’s just this gap. \n\nThere is a whole chunk in the middle of the event where I know I was there, obviously, because I'm sitting here talking to you now, but I just... I can’t pull it back. It’s like the system went offline and just stopped writing data for a few minutes. I try, I really do. I'll be lying in bed, or sitting at my desk when I'm supposed to be running reports, just trying to force the timeline to make sense, trying to connect point A to point C. But point B is just a black hole. \n\nI know time passed, I know things happened in that middle chunk, but no matter how hard I try to query my own brain, I get nothing. A complete retrieval error. It drives me absolutely crazy because I hate not knowing, I hate losing control of the data. I hate that my own mind just seemingly deleted or encrypted a portion of my life, even if it was a terrible portion. I know I was there, but I just can't access it no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I know what you're asking, like having a firewall constantly pinging for intrusions or running a broad-spectrum threat scan in the background all day, but that's not really how my brain is operating right now. \n\nWhen I'm in what I consider my safe zones—like my living room at home, or even just sitting quietly at my specific workstation, assuming I took my usual safe route to get there and completely avoided the west wing—I actually feel fine. Secure, even. I'm not sitting there looking over my shoulder every five seconds, and I don't check the locks on my front door three or four times at night to make sure nobody is coming in. \n\nIt's really more about avoiding the known bad sectors, like I was explaining before with the hallways and the conversations. If I'm in a controlled environment, I just want to put my head down and focus on my tasks. I don't have this generalized, spinning radar looking for new, random dangers out in the wild. Like, if I'm at the grocery store on a Sunday afternoon, I'm just comparing prices on coffee beans or looking at produce. I'm not calculating the fastest escape routes out of the dairy aisle, and I'm not eyeing every single person who walks past my cart thinking they're going to rush at me. I just want to buy my groceries and go home in peace. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout everywhere I go. My... my threat detection, I guess you could call it, is just highly, highly specific to that one night and the things directly associated with it. As long as I'm nowhere near that parking lot, and nobody is getting animated on a conference call or talking about the overnight contractor crew, my system isn't actively searching for danger. I just try to stay in my lane and keep things as predictable as humanly possible.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since that night?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, absolutely. It's... it's a complete mess right now. You know how you're supposed to just power down at the end of the day? Close out all the background applications and just rest? My brain just refuses to execute that command. I'll get into bed, usually around 11:00, which used to be my standard, reliable time, but instead of drifting off, I just lie there. And I mean I lie there for hours. Staring at the ceiling, trying to focus on the ceiling fan or the dark room, but my mind just starts running these unwanted processes, constantly replaying pieces of that office incident over and over again. It's like a log file stuck in an infinite loop, just cycling through the details of what happened, turning it over and over. \n\nAnd then, when my system finally does crash—when I actually manage to fall asleep just out of sheer physical exhaustion—it's not even a restful state. It's totally fragmented. I'll jolt awake, completely tense, my shoulders up by my ears, my muscles just locked up. Sometimes it's because of a dream, though I don't always remember the exact narrative, just that awful, lingering feeling of panic. Other times, it's just from some tiny, insignificant noise in the house. The ice maker in the fridge dropping ice, the HVAC kicking on, or just the floorboards settling. Just a tiny little ping in the environment, and I'm instantly awake again, completely wired and staring at the clock. \n\nBecause of all that, by the time my actual alarm goes off in the morning, I am just utterly depleted. I'm exhausted before work even starts. I have to drag myself out of bed and try to force down coffee just to get enough baseline energy to look at my monitors and do my job. But it's like starting the workday with my battery already at five percent. It's incredibly frustrating because I know I need the downtime to function properly, to process data and solve problems, but my sleep cycle is just completely broken since that night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So, no, I'm definitely not watching myself from the outside. I'm very much stuck on the inside, feeling every awful bit of it. \n\nIt's like I'm hyper-connected to my body, not disconnected. I almost wish I could just unplug and float away sometimes, honestly, because feeling all that adrenaline and terror rushing through my veins every time I get reminded of that night is just exhausting. It just drains my battery down to zero, having to physically feel all of that over and over again. But no, I've never had that watching-myself feeling. I'm always just right here.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of that night?\n\nPatient: Yes, yes I do. It's... it's honestly one of the most disruptive parts of my day. You know, I was talking earlier about how I try to avoid that side of the building, the conference rooms and all that? Well, sometimes I just can't avoid it, or sometimes it doesn't even matter where I am, it just happens completely out of nowhere. Like, absolutely unprompted. No warning, no error message, just—boom. \n\nI'll be sitting there, maybe in that specific conference area if I absolutely had to go in for a mandatory all-hands, or even just sitting safely at my own desk on a routine conference call. And if someone... if someone just raises their voice on the call, you know? Not even yelling at me, just getting a little too animated about a project deadline or a server patch or whatever, it's like a switch flips in my head. \n\nSuddenly, I get hit with these images and sounds from that night, and it's not just like casually remembering something, it's so incredibly strong. It's like I'm right back in the lot. I hear the gravel crunching, I see the orange streetlight on his jacket, I hear that awful noise of him rushing at me—all those things I told you about earlier, they just flood my system all at once. It's so intense, so loud and vivid, that it completely overrides whatever is actually happening in reality. \n\nAnd I'll be right in the middle of a sentence, explaining a system architecture or giving a status update to my team, and I just... I completely lose track of what I was saying. My mind just drops the connection to the present because it's so overwhelmed by the images from the parking lot. I'll just stop talking, right mid-sentence. And then there's this awful, awkward silence on the call, and people are saying, \"Hello? Are you still there? Did we lose you?\" And I have to scramble to pretend it was a network lag or my headset disconnected, because how do you explain to your project managers that you just spent the last ten seconds terrified out of your mind because of a sound only you can hear? It's just so frustrating, this constant intrusion into my work. I just want to do my job, but my brain keeps forcing these pop-ups of that night that I can't close, and it takes me so long to reboot and figure out where I even was in the conversation.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid expressing or feeling certain emotions because they remind you of that night?\n\nPatient: Emotions? Well... I guess, yes, but it's... it's all tied together with what I was telling you earlier. I don't know if I consciously say to myself, \"I'm going to avoid feeling scared today,\" but I do absolutely everything in my power to prevent those specific feelings from bubbling up. Because when they do, it's just too much. It's a complete system overload. \n\nSo, to keep those emotions offline, to stop myself from feeling that absolute, paralyzing terror and vulnerability again, I have to rely on all those workarounds I mentioned. That's exactly why I take the long way around the building, you know? And why I avoid that floor whenever I possibly can. It's not just about the physical space itself, it's about avoiding the intense dread that completely washes over me if I get even a few feet too close to that side of the campus. If I don't see the gravel, if I don't look out those windows, I don't have to feel the panic. I can just keep it suppressed. \n\nAnd it's the exact same principle with the people at work and the things they say. That's the whole reason I shut down conversations the moment people start talking about the contractor or the cutover. I mean, the absolute second the words leave their mouths, I have to stop it. Because if I let them keep talking, if I just sit there politely and listen to them complain about the overnight shift or the HVAC repairs, I'm going to start feeling all of it again. My chest will tighten, and all that fear just comes rushing back in. \n\nI just can't afford to feel those things while I'm trying to do my job, while I'm trying to run diagnostics or lead a project update. It's just not sustainable to be emotionally compromised like that in a professional environment. So, I guess my way of avoiding the emotions is by rigorously avoiding the triggers. It's like I have to perfectly control my environment, down to the exact hallway I walk down and the exact topics my coworkers are allowed to discuss around me, just to keep those feelings locked away in a box. Because if the box opens... well, I just can't let it open. So yes, I avoid the feelings by strictly policing where I go and what I listen to, even though it takes up so much of my mental bandwidth every single day.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering important parts of what happened that night?\n\nPatient: Yes. Actually, yes, and it is incredibly frustrating for someone in my line of work. Because, you know, my entire job is about tracking logs, finding the root cause, mapping out processes from start to finish without any dropped packets. But with that night... it's like there's a corrupted sector in my memory. \n\nI can remember some parts of what happened so, so clearly. I mean, painfully clearly. Like I said before, the noise of that gravel crunching, the exact look on the contractor’s face, the way the orange light hit him when he rushed at me... those details are burned into my retinas like a monitor that’s been left on too long. I can see and hear all of that in absolute high definition. It's perfectly preserved. But then... then there’s just this gap. \n\nThere is a whole chunk in the middle of the event where I know I was there, obviously, because I'm sitting here talking to you now, but I just... I can’t pull it back. It’s like the system went offline and just stopped writing data for a few minutes. I try, I really do. I'll be lying in bed, or sitting at my desk when I'm supposed to be running reports, just trying to force the timeline to make sense, trying to connect point A to point C. But point B is just a black hole. \n\nI know time passed, I know things happened in that middle chunk, but no matter how hard I try to query my own brain, I get nothing. A complete retrieval error. It drives me absolutely crazy because I hate not knowing, I hate losing control of the data. I hate that my own mind just seemingly deleted or encrypted a portion of my life, even if it was a terrible portion. I know I was there, but I just can't access it no matter what I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: No, I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I know what you're asking, like having a firewall constantly pinging for intrusions or running a broad-spectrum threat scan in the background all day, but that's not really how my brain is operating right now. \n\nWhen I'm in what I consider my safe zones—like my living room at home, or even just sitting quietly at my specific workstation, assuming I took my usual safe route to get there and completely avoided the west wing—I actually feel fine. Secure, even. I'm not sitting there looking over my shoulder every five seconds, and I don't check the locks on my front door three or four times at night to make sure nobody is coming in. \n\nIt's really more about avoiding the known bad sectors, like I was explaining before with the hallways and the conversations. If I'm in a controlled environment, I just want to put my head down and focus on my tasks. I don't have this generalized, spinning radar looking for new, random dangers out in the wild. Like, if I'm at the grocery store on a Sunday afternoon, I'm just comparing prices on coffee beans or looking at produce. I'm not calculating the fastest escape routes out of the dairy aisle, and I'm not eyeing every single person who walks past my cart thinking they're going to rush at me. I just want to buy my groceries and go home in peace. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm constantly on the lookout everywhere I go. My... my threat detection, I guess you could call it, is just highly, highly specific to that one night and the things directly associated with it. As long as I'm nowhere near that parking lot, and nobody is getting animated on a conference call or talking about the overnight contractor crew, my system isn't actively searching for danger. I just try to stay in my lane and keep things as predictable as humanly possible.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since that night?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, absolutely. It's... it's a complete mess right now. You know how you're supposed to just power down at the end of the day? Close out all the background applications and just rest? My brain just refuses to execute that command. I'll get into bed, usually around 11:00, which used to be my standard, reliable time, but instead of drifting off, I just lie there. And I mean I lie there for hours. Staring at the ceiling, trying to focus on the ceiling fan or the dark room, but my mind just starts running these unwanted processes, constantly replaying pieces of that office incident over and over again. It's like a log file stuck in an infinite loop, just cycling through the details of what happened, turning it over and over. \n\nAnd then, when my system finally does crash—when I actually manage to fall asleep just out of sheer physical exhaustion—it's not even a restful state. It's totally fragmented. I'll jolt awake, completely tense, my shoulders up by my ears, my muscles just locked up. Sometimes it's because of a dream, though I don't always remember the exact narrative, just that awful, lingering feeling of panic. Other times, it's just from some tiny, insignificant noise in the house. The ice maker in the fridge dropping ice, the HVAC kicking on, or just the floorboards settling. Just a tiny little ping in the environment, and I'm instantly awake again, completely wired and staring at the clock. \n\nBecause of all that, by the time my actual alarm goes off in the morning, I am just utterly depleted. I'm exhausted before work even starts. I have to drag myself out of bed and try to force down coffee just to get enough baseline energy to look at my monitors and do my job. But it's like starting the workday with my battery already at five percent. It's incredibly frustrating because I know I need the downtime to function properly, to process data and solve problems, but my sleep cycle is just completely broken since that night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just want to live my life and do my job without feeling like I'm under attack, and for the most part, outside of those specific triggers, I can do that.
